## fix(signing): peer verification withholds unverified agent entries and binds the conversation, position and key version (2026-11-08)

**Repo:** EDDI (`fix/group-signing-verification`, on top of `feat/agent-signing-key-provisioning`)

### What changed and why

`security.requirePeerVerification` verified earlier speakers' signatures and then handed the
receiving agent the transcript anyway: every failure was a log line. A signature also proved less
than it seemed. It covered only the content and the phase name, so a signed entry copied into
another discussion, into another phase or round of the same one, or retyped, still verified; the
verifier checked no nonce, so a duplicated entry verified twice; and key validity windows were
never enforced.

- **Withheld, for a member that verifies.** `GroupSigningGuard.viewFor` gives one verdict per transcript entry
  (`VERIFIED`, `NOT_AGENT_AUTHORED`, `RECEIVERS_OWN`, `UNSIGNED`, `UNKNOWN_KEY`, `REVOKED`,
  `OUT_OF_WINDOW`, `BAD_SIGNATURE`, `WRONG_ROUND`, `DUPLICATE`, `ENGINE_SYNTHESIS`, `NO_SUCH_AGENT`). For a member
  that requires peer verification, every agent entry that must verify and does not is replaced, in place, by a
  `[withheld: ... because ...]` placeholder before that member's phase prompt
  (`GroupContextBuilder.buildPhaseInput`) and its `groupTranscript` context (`MemberTurnExecutor`)
  are built. In place, so the raw indices window summaries are keyed on stay aligned. The
  placeholder names the speaker by its agent id and has no phase name, since the stored display
  name and phase name are as unverified as the content. Each withheld
  entry is logged at ERROR the first time per conversation, then at DEBUG. A record with no
  content (a skip, an error, a pass; verdicts `STATUS_RECORD` and `NON_AGENT_STATUS_RECORD`) is
  unsigned by design, so it keeps its place, speaker id, type, phase index and target but loses its
  error reason, display name and phase name, which a store writer could otherwise fill under a
  member agent's id for a verifying member's `groupTranscript` to carry.
  This is not fail-closed in general, and is not described so any more: a member without
  `requirePeerVerification` (the default, unchanged) gets the stored transcript with nothing checked
  and nothing logged; for a verifying member, entries no agent wrote (`NOT_AGENT_AUTHORED`) and its
  own when it does not sign (`RECEIVERS_OWN`) are shown, and the engine's other prompts and a
  follow-up still quote the stored transcript (see "Not filtered yet" below). An agent entry that is
  unsigned or names an undeclared key is withheld only from that member's phase prompt and
  `groupTranscript`. The `Verdict` enum now has one constant per line, each with a Javadoc line
  saying whether an entry with it is shown or withheld.
- **The member's own entries** are checked like a peer's when it signs: every honest one is signed,
  and REVISION and DEFENSE quote its latest entry from the group transcript as the answer it revises
  or defends, so an answer planted under its id, or its own signed answer edited in the store, is
  withheld instead. A verifying member that does not sign (the validator allows it; a moderator is
  the usual case) has nothing to check them against and is shown them as stored, `RECEIVERS_OWN`;
  without that it was being asked to revise or defend a placeholder.
- **What the signature covers.** The payload is built in one place for signing and verification:
  the group conversation id, the round (stored on the entry as the new `signatureRound`), the phase
  index and name, the entry type, the content, the speaker's display name, the target agent, the
  error reason and the key version (the envelope's own `keyVersion` is outside its canonical form).
  A reply the engine records as a `DISSENT` or a `BID` is signed again as that type
  (`MemberTurnExecutor.sign`). Unpaired UTF-16 surrogates become U+FFFD before signing, and text
  holding one never verifies: Java's UTF-8 encoder writes one as `?`, so it signed the same bytes as
  the text with `?`, and a store writing real UTF-8 turned an honest entry into a failure.
- **Replay.** A nonce is accepted once per transcript; only an occurrence that verifies claims it,
  so a forgery carrying a genuine nonce cannot get the genuine entry withheld. Rounds only grow along
  the transcript, and the current round starts at `roundStartTranscriptIndex`, so an entry moved
  into another round is withheld (`WRONG_ROUND`). Moving one between repeats of a phase within a
  round is not detected. An entry signed before its conversation began (30 s skew) is withheld; that
  rule rests on the stored `created` time.
- **Revocation and windows.** A key revoked through the new admin endpoint (base branch) vouches for
  nothing, whatever time an entry claims (`REVOKED`); the signer does not sign with a revoked newest
  key. A `validFromMs`/`validUntilMs` window is enforced on both sides, but against the time the
  signer wrote, so it limits honest use and does not stop a leaked key. Revocation applies to every
  prompt built after it; what a member was already shown stays in its own conversation history,
  which its model sees again until the history window drops it (documented; a new discussion
  clears it).
- **Deleted agents.** Whether a speaker is an agent was decided by whether the agent store had a
  current version of it. A soft delete (MongoDB throws, PostgreSQL returns null) or a permanent one
  therefore turned every entry of that agent into a non-agent's: shown relabelled instead of
  withheld. So revoking a compromised agent's key and then deleting it, the ordinary response,
  put its revoked entries back in front of verifying members. Now an entry under an id with no
  current agent is withheld (`NO_SUCH_AGENT`) when it carries a signature, which only agents give,
  or when an agent turn of the discussion ran under that id (its member conversation is recorded
  under it); a human member's or a nested group's entry still passes labelled. The placeholder
  names the id followed by `(not an agent, unverified)`: the id is whatever the entry was stored
  under, which no agent store vouches for, so a planted one cannot put unlabelled text in the
  speaker's place. A deleted agent's
  genuine signed entries are withheld too: the trust anchor is the speaker's current
  configuration, and a deleted agent has none.
- **Non-agent entries.** Which entries must verify is decided by the speaker id, while a prompt
  shows the display name; a store writer could put any text under an agent's name by giving it a
  non-agent id. A verifying member now sees such entries as `User`, `System` or
  `<name> (not an agent, unverified)`, and a group save refuses those names for an agent member.
  Under `ANONYMOUS` scope (Delphi's later rounds), where every speaker is `Anonymous`, such an entry
  is `Anonymous (not an agent, unverified)` in the prompt and in `groupTranscript`; before, the
  scope dropped the label and a store writer's `system` opinion read like a verified peer's.
  Group saves also refuse two members with the same display name (ignoring case), and an agent
  member named `Moderator` while the moderator is not a member or `Facilitator` while an enabled
  facilitator is another agent, the names the engine signs those roles' entries under; so two
  verified voices never share one name either.
- **Synthesis under the engine's name.** A synthesis is looked up by its type and quoted without its
  speaker (the dissent prompt, the vote's options), so the relabelling did not reach it. The engine
  never records one under `system`, `user` or no speaker, and a verifying member now has such an
  entry withheld (`ENGINE_SYNTHESIS`). A reviewer's edit and a human or nested-group moderator's
  synthesis are unsigned by nature and still shown.
- **Summary and ballots.** The stored window summary is free text no signature covers, so a
  verifying member gets the truncation marker instead. A ballot lists the options the tally counts,
  resolved from the stored transcript: a verifying voter from whom the synthesis was withheld got
  no options, could not cast a parseable ballot, and counted against quorum.
- **Unreadable receiver.** If a member's configuration cannot be read, the setting this node last
  read for it applies; if it never read one, the member's turn is recorded `SKIPPED` instead of
  running on a transcript filtered for a setting nobody knows. Before, every member, verifying or
  not, was treated as verifying. The check runs before the member conversation is started, since
  the first turn with a conversation is the one that is given the discussion's attachments.
- **Cost.** Each Ed25519 check is remembered (Caffeine, by SHA-256 of key, signature and signed
  bytes), so a verifying member no longer re-runs it over the whole transcript twice per turn; the
  revocation and window are re-checked each time. Agent configurations are read per version through
  an `AgentConfigurationCache`. The per-conversation log de-duplication is a bounded cache, so a
  conversation that pauses on one node and ends on another no longer leaks its entry.
- **Signer.** `signOutgoingMessage(...)` became `sign(gc, entry)`. It no longer registers its own
  nonce with `NonceCacheService`: a freshly minted UUID can never collide, and with
  `eddi.cluster.degraded.nonces=reject` a NATS outage silently turned signing off. Nothing calls
  that service any more, so its two `eddi.a2a.signing.nonce.*` properties do nothing
  ([configuration reference](../configuration-reference.md) and
  [clustering](../clustering.md) say so); deleting the class is a follow-up. A signing agent that
  cannot sign now logs at ERROR, since peers that verify will withhold the entry.
- **Docs, Manager, operator.** [`architecture.md`](../architecture.md#cryptographic-agent-identity)
  (signing, replay, revocation, peer verification; "non-repudiation" dropped, since the deployment holds every key; the RFC 8785
  claim corrected) and a [Signed contributions](../group-conversations.md#signed-contributions)
  section. The Manager's "Require peer verification" description no longer says failures are only
  logged (all 11 locales). The revoke dialog says again that peers stop trusting every message
  signed with the revoked key: the base branch's copy says peer verification does not refuse a
  revoked versioned key yet, which this branch makes untrue; its best-effort vault delete and
  legacy-v0 wording stay (all 11 locales). The Platform Operator's prompt says what a verifying agent sees and who
  must sign (operator revision bumped).

Nothing had been signed in the wild (provisioning only lands with the base branch), so the change
of the signed bytes needs no migration.

### Upgrading

A group whose members share a display name, or with an agent member named `User`, `System` or
`... (not an agent, unverified)`, or `Moderator` / `Facilitator` as above, is refused on its next
save until the names change; stored groups still load and run. A verifying member in a windowed
discussion no longer gets the rolling summary. A member that both signs and verifies has its own
unsigned entries (a signing failure, or answers from before signing was switched on) withheld from
itself, as its verifying peers already do. A continued discussion withholds from verifying members
every entry of a member agent that has since been deleted.

### Not filtered yet

The prompts the engine writes itself for role turns (convergence judge, facilitator briefing, vote
tie-break, dissent round, task-force plan, execute, verify and bid) quote the round just run, task
records or a short excerpt directly. Agent follow-up answers, facilitator interventions and the
reformatted task-force verification entry are recorded unsigned, so a verifying member sees them
withheld.

A follow-up (`followUpWithMember`) is not filtered either: the member it asks gets the stored
transcript unfiltered in its `groupTranscript` context, even when it requires peer verification, so
an entry altered by someone with write access to the store reaches it as written.

The [group-conversations](../group-conversations.md#signed-contributions) page now names
these limits too: an entry's stored `timestamp` is not covered by its signature, an earlier round's
signed entry can be moved past a later round's question without being detected, and a group saved
before display names had to be unique keeps loading with its duplicate names until it is saved
again. The operator prompt merged from the key-provisioning branch said peer verification ignores
revocation; on this branch it does not, and the prompt says so.

**Files:** [`GroupSigningGuard.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/internal/groups/GroupSigningGuard.java),
[`GroupContextBuilder.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/internal/groups/GroupContextBuilder.java),
[`MemberTurnExecutor.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/internal/groups/MemberTurnExecutor.java),
[`PhaseExecutionEngine.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/internal/groups/PhaseExecutionEngine.java),
[`TaskForceEngine.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/internal/groups/TaskForceEngine.java),

## 🐛 fix(manager): saves and previews do not overwrite edits made while in flight (2026-11-08)

**Repo:** EDDI (`fix/manager-form-state`)

### What changed and why

Four Manager forms let the admin keep working while a request was running, then
applied the reply (or, for the transfer dialog, a Cancel) to whatever the form had
become by then.

- **Quotas** — a successful save replaced the form with the server's copy and
  cleared the dirty flag, so a limit typed during the PUT vanished and the
  unsaved-changes guard stopped protecting it. The page now counts edits; the
  server copy is taken only when nothing was edited since sending, otherwise the
  form keeps the edit and stays dirty.
- **Connection detail** — the same on success, plus a second loss: following the
  new version reset the form and re-seeded it from the cache. The save now moves
  the baseline to what it stored, keeps the draft when the form (or uncommitted
  chip text) changed since sending, and tells the route-change reset to leave that
  draft alone for exactly the version it created. The edit stays unsaved and the
  next save goes against the new version. Text typed into a parameter row that has no
  name yet counts as a change too, though such a row is not part of the document.
- **Agent sync** — a batch preview landed on rows whose target or "Create new"
  choice had changed during the request (a diff for one target shown under
  another, with Sync armed to send the new one), or that a reconnect had relisted
  at a newer source version, and a row ticked mid-flight was marked "Preview
  failed". A preview now applies only to a row still checked and still naming the
  source version and target it was sent with.
- **Team budget transfer** — Cancel always started a fresh `Idempotency-Key` and
  an empty form, so after a 5xx or network error (which may have moved the money)
  re-entering the same transfer could move it twice. After such an error, or when
  the dialog is closed while the transfer is still running, closing keeps the
  values, the error and the key; a success or a 4xx starts a new transfer. The table's dead `initialFromId` prop is gone (its only opener passed
  `""`), and its open state is a boolean.

### Design decisions

- Snapshot-and-compare rather than locking the fields while saving: the forms
  stay usable, and nothing typed is thrown away. Quotas uses an edit counter
  because a half-typed number changes the dirty flag without changing the form.
- No new component or hook; each fix stays in the file that owns the state.

**Files:** [`quotas.tsx`](../../ui/manager/src/pages/quotas.tsx),
[`connection-detail.tsx`](../../ui/manager/src/pages/connection-detail.tsx),
[`sync-page.tsx`](../../ui/manager/src/pages/sync-page.tsx),
[`team-budget-dialogs.tsx`](../../ui/packages/app-kit/src/components/quotas/team-budget-dialogs.tsx),
[`team-budgets-table.tsx`](../../ui/manager/src/components/quotas/team-budgets-table.tsx),
[`team-budgets.md`](../team-budgets.md)

## 🐛 fix(rag): storage collision check refuses on unreadable neighbours; cluster-shared ingestion status (2026-11-08)

**Repo:** EDDI (`fix/rag-store-guards`)

### What changed and why

**The explicit-location collision check now fails closed when another knowledge base cannot be read.**
`KnowledgeBaseStorageGuard` refuses an explicit `table` / `collectionName` / `indexName` that another
knowledge base already uses unless the caller may edit that one too. It already failed closed when the
scan ran past its page budget, but a neighbour whose document could not be read (a `ResourceStoreException`
or any runtime error while loading it) was logged at debug level and skipped. The neighbour that cannot be
read may be the very one that owns the location, so a transient read error let an editor point their
knowledge base at another knowledge base's table. Such a save now fails with the same `IllegalStateException`
the descriptor-listing failure already raised (a 500), naming the knowledge base that could not be read. A
neighbour that is gone (`ResourceNotFoundException`, deleted since it was listed) still owns no location and
is still skipped.

**Ingestion status is shared across a cluster.** `RagIngestionService` kept the status and warning of each
`POST /ragstore/rags/{id}/ingest` in two private Caffeine caches, so with `eddi.messaging.type=nats` a poll of
`GET …/ingestion/{ingestionId}/status` that a load balancer sent to another node answered 404 "no ingestion
with this id is known" for an ingestion that was running or done. Both now live in one cache from
`ICacheFactory`, `rag-ingestion-status` (one-hour TTL, sized 20,000: a status and at most one warning per
ingestion), which `CacheFactory.SHARED_BUCKETS` maps to the new `RAG_INGESTION` KV bucket. A single node
behaves exactly as before.

**A value written while NATS was down no longer reads back as the older shared value, and never overwrites a newer one.** `SharedKvCache.get()` only consulted the node-local copy when the bucket answered empty, so a key written `processing` with NATS up and `completed` during an outage kept reading `processing` after recovery, even on the writing node. A degraded write now remembers the bucket revision this node last saw for the key (or that the key was absent, or that it is unknown), and the next read of the key publishes the local value **by compare-and-set** against that revision (`update`, or `create` for an absent key). If another node wrote or deleted the key meanwhile, the bucket's state wins, the marker and the stale local copy are dropped and nothing is overwritten or resurrected; an unknown revision yields to a present bucket value and creates an absent one. A value the bucket refuses as too large (`ClusterValueRefusedException`, a subclass of `ClusterUnavailableException` thrown by `NatsSharedKv` for a payload or message size refusal) is tracked apart and never retried, so the read path costs no failed network write: it behaves as before, answering from the local copy only while the bucket holds nothing. Republishing starts a new bucket TTL (the KV API cannot preserve the original expiry), so a status written during an outage can stay readable for up to about twice its TTL after the last update; the writing node's own copy expires after one TTL, so nothing older is ever republished. Applies to all shared caches.

### Design decisions

- **One cache and one bucket, two key kinds.** The status is kept under `status:` plus the ingestion id
  and the warning under `warning:` plus the id, so neither can overwrite the other in the bucket, and an id
  polled as `warning:<id>` finds no status. Two caches in two buckets would add a JetStream stream for a
  handful of strings per ingestion.
- **A status is bound to its knowledge base.** The status endpoint checks access on the RAG configuration
  in its path, but an ingestion id is global, so an editor of knowledge base B holding an id of A could poll
  it through B and read A's document name or failure message (on any node, once the cache is shared). Both
  keys now carry the RAG configuration id (`status:<ragConfigId>:<id>`), so an id polled through another
  knowledge base is unknown (404). No extra NATS writes.
- **No retry timer after a NATS outage.** If NATS is unreachable while the terminal status is written, that write stays on the originating node, like every other non-fail-closed shared cache. It is published when that node next reads the key (compare-and-set, see above); no background retry is added here.
- **500, not 400, for an unreadable neighbour.** The request is not wrong; the server could not check it.
  It matches the listing-failure path right beside it, and a retry succeeds once the store answers.
- **Not in this PR:** serialising the check and the write (two editors saving the same new location at the
  same moment both pass). A per-JVM lock does not serialise a cluster, the import and upgrade paths write
  outside `RestRagStore`, and in the per-id layout, which every new knowledge base gets, chunks in a shared
  table are still filtered by knowledge-base id (a knowledge base still in the 6.5.0 name layout is not
  filtered, so for one of those the race is a real read). Injecting the
  guard into `RestRagStore` instead of constructing it is only a step toward that lock, so it waits for it.
  The replace locks in `RagIngestionService` stay per instance, as their Javadoc says.

**Files:** [`KnowledgeBaseStorageGuard.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/configs/rag/rest/KnowledgeBaseStorageGuard.java),
[`RagIngestionService.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/modules/rag/RagIngestionService.java),
[`CacheFactory.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/caching/CacheFactory.java),
[`SharedKvCache.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/caching/SharedKvCache.java),
[`ClusterValueRefusedException.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/cluster/ClusterValueRefusedException.java),
[`rag.md`](../rag.md), [`clustering.md`](../clustering.md); tests in `KnowledgeBaseStorageGuardTest`,
`RagIngestionServiceTest`, `SharedKvCacheTest` and `CacheFactoryTest`.

## fix(datastore): Mongo id queries fetch only id and version; deletes lock per id instead of per store (2026-11-08)

**Repo:** EDDI (`fix/resource-storage-small`)

### What changed and why

- **MongoDB id queries project `_id` and `_version` only.**
  `MongoResourceStorage.findResources` returns `(id, version)` pairs. The
  listings read the documents again by id (descriptor listings through
  `readMany`, the group conversation and shared artifact listings through
  `read`), and the GDPR erasure loops only need the ids. Without a projection,
  every match crossed the wire and was decoded for nothing, whole transcripts
  and artifact bodies included, and a listed one was decoded twice. `findResources`,
  `findResourceIdsContaining` and `getCurrentVersion` now project
  `{_version: 1}` (`_id` comes back unless excluded), and
  `findHistoryResourceIdsContaining` projects `{_id: 1}`, the composite id that
  carries the version. `getCurrentVersion` is behind
  `HistorizedResourceStore.getCurrentResourceId`, which has about 150 callers.
  Filtering and sorting still run on the server over the full documents. The
  PostgreSQL twin already selected only these columns.
- **`readHistoryLatest` makes one round trip instead of two.** It ran
  `countDocuments` and then the same filter as a sorted `limit(1)` find. The
  find returns null on its own when there is no history row, so the count is
  gone. The history document is still read in full, because callers of
  `IHistoryResource` can call `getData()`.
- **`HistorizedResourceStore` locks per resource id instead of per store.**
  `delete` and `deleteAllPermanently` were `synchronized` on the store, and
  there is one store instance per resource type, so on one node every delete of
  that type (for example the GDPR erasure loop over conversation descriptors)
  waited for the one in progress, which held the monitor across several
  majority-acknowledged round trips. The monitor was not useless, though: on
  MongoDB without a transaction it was the only thing on a node that kept a
  `delete` from reading a resource, losing the CPU while `deleteAllPermanently`
  erased it, and then upserting its tombstone (the erased data) into the emptied
  history after the erasure had reported success. It did not cover the same race
  through `update`, which archives the predecessor before its version-checked
  replace and was never locked. Now `update`, `delete` and
  `deleteAllPermanently` take one of 64 `ReentrantLock` stripes, chosen by
  `Math.floorMod(id.hashCode(), 64)`, for their whole read-then-write sequence.
  Same-id sequences on a node run one after the other, different ids contend
  only when they share a stripe, and each call holds one stripe and calls only
  the storage under it, so stripes are never nested. **What it does not
  guarantee:** the lock is per JVM, so a delete or update on one node and an
  erasure of the same id on another can still meet on MongoDB, exactly as they
  could under `synchronized`; `ModifiableHistorizedResourceStore.set` is not
  locked (its writes are version-checked replaces with no upsert, so they cannot
  write into an erased id); and stores that call `IResourceStorage.removeAllPermanently`
  directly (group conversations, shared artifacts, workspaces, agent test runs)
  bypass it. Two deletes of the same id, and update against delete, are decided
  by the storage's version checks either way.

### Design decisions

- `readHistoryLatest` keeps reading the full document (no projection), since
  the history resource it returns is a data accessor.
- A striped lock rather than no lock or a map of per-id locks: removing the
  monitor outright reopened the MongoDB erasure race on a single node, and a
  `ConcurrentHashMap` of locks grows with every id ever touched. 64 stripes are
  a fixed 64 locks per store.
- **PostgreSQL `storeHistoryAndRemove` now deletes first and writes the tombstone
  only if the delete removed the row.** The old order (tombstone, then delete)
  let a `deleteAllPermanently` that finished between a delete's read and its
  write be undone: the tombstone was upserted over the erased id, and the
  "no live row, a concurrent delete won" branch committed it, leaving the erased
  resource's data in `resources_history`. The monitor used to prevent that on
  one node, and the per-id lock still does; ordering the statements prevents it
  on every node, because the
  delete takes the row lock (an erasure waits for it and then also deletes the
  tombstone, or it has run first and the delete writes nothing). The same
  reordering means a refused delete (an update moved the resource on) never
  writes a tombstone to roll back.
- **MongoDB keeps tombstone-then-delete and has a known residual window.**
  Without a transaction the tombstone write and the check that the row still
  exists cannot be one step, and "row gone" cannot tell a concurrent delete from
  an erasure. The `MongoResourceStorage.storeHistoryAndRemove` Javadoc states
  the limit; on one node the per-id lock closes it, across nodes it stays open,
  and a repeated erasure removes the orphan row.

**Files:** [`MongoResourceStorage.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/datastore/mongo/MongoResourceStorage.java),
[`PostgresResourceStorage.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/datastore/postgres/PostgresResourceStorage.java),
[`HistorizedResourceStore.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/datastore/HistorizedResourceStore.java),
[`PostgresResourceStorageTest.java`](../../src/test/java/ai/labs/eddi/datastore/postgres/PostgresResourceStorageTest.java),
[`MongoResourceStorageTest.java`](../../src/test/java/ai/labs/eddi/datastore/mongo/MongoResourceStorageTest.java),
[`MongoResourceStorageBranchTest.java`](../../src/test/java/ai/labs/eddi/datastore/mongo/MongoResourceStorageBranchTest.java),
[`HistorizedResourceStoreTest.java`](../../src/test/java/ai/labs/eddi/datastore/HistorizedResourceStoreTest.java)

## feat(signing): identity is engine-owned, keys can be revoked, and generating one no longer breaks the agent's descriptor (2026-11-08)

**Repo:** EDDI (`feat/agent-signing-key-provisioning`, review round on the same branch)

### What changed and why

A whole-feature review of agent signing found the provisioning endpoint broke the agent it
provisioned, and that the keys it wrote were not the trust anchor they looked like.

- **Generating a key keeps the descriptor in step.** `createSigningKey` wrote the new agent version
  in process and answered `201`. `DocumentDescriptorFilter` takes a POST answered `201` for a
  creation: it tried to create a second descriptor for the agent, failed on the duplicate key and
  answered 500 — with the key stored and the agent already at the new version. The descriptor stayed
  at the old version, so every later save answered 404 and a conversation on any newer deployed
  version could not start. The endpoint now moves the descriptor itself (as
  `UpgradeExecutor.bumpDescriptor` does) and answers `200` with `Location`.
- **`identity` is engine-owned.** Any `eddi-editor` could `PUT` `identity.keys[]` — plant a key
  whose private half they hold (a second `v1` placed first wins the version lookup), delete a
  revocation, or drop the identity so a permanent delete swept nothing — while only minting was
  admin-only. `RestAgentStore.updateAgent` now keeps the stored `identity` for every caller (a
  `PUT`, an import merge, a sync, the cascade, autonomy), as it already did for `dynamicOrigin`, and
  `createAgent` stores none. Keys change only through the admin endpoints.
- **New: `POST /agentstore/agents/{id}/signing-keys/{keyVersion}/revoke?version=N`**
  (`eddi-admin`). Marks every entry of that version `revoked: true` (a new field on
  `AgentPublicKey`, default `false`; a revoked key is valid at no time) in a new compatible version,
  moves the descriptor, then deletes the private key from the vault. `keyVersion` 0 removes the
  legacy `identity.publicKey` and its vault entry, and revokes an `identity.keys` entry declared as
  version 0 (malformed, but what a peer checks a v0 signature against). A negative version, which
  no vault entry can have, deletes nothing: it used to be read as the legacy key, whose private half
  the agent may still need. Already revoked: `200`, nothing written, but the private key is still
  deleted — that delete is best effort after the write, so revoking again is how a key a degraded
  vault kept is retried. A write whose outcome is unknown (a socket error or a lost acknowledgement,
  which can arrive after the write was applied) is handled as generating a key handles it: the agent
  is read again, and if its current version no longer trusts the key the revoke landed — the
  descriptor moves, the private key is deleted and the answer is `200`. Otherwise the failure is
  rethrown and the key kept, since it is still declared.
- **The save-time check covers every declared key.** With `signInterAgentMessages` on, a save is
  refused (400) when a key that is not revoked is malformed (version outside 1 to 1 000 000, a
  version declared twice, not an Ed25519 public key), or when the newest key is revoked or outside
  its `validFromMs`/`validUntilMs` window. Versions outside that range are ignored when numbering, so
  a declared `Integer.MAX_VALUE` no longer wraps the next key to v1, and `generateKey` can no longer
  overflow its search into a 503 that blamed the vault.
- **A config write whose outcome is unknown no longer discards a key that landed.** Only a refusal
  (`ResourceModifiedException`, `ResourceNotFoundException`) discards at once; after any other
  failure the agent is re-read, the key is kept and the call answers as a success if the current
  version declares it, and it is kept if the agent cannot be read.
- **Import and sync never carry signing onto an agent that cannot sign.** The target's identity is
  never replaced, so a merge import or sync that would write `signInterAgentMessages: true` onto an
  agent whose own newest key is not usable writes it off instead of being refused and rolled back,
  and says so: `X-Agent-Signing-Turned-Off: <reason>` on an import (also on a create, whose new agent
  has no key), `warnings` in the sync's `UpgradeResult` (a new, normally absent field). New
  non-endpoint `IRestAgentStore.signingKeyProblem(id)` answers the question for both. The Manager
  shows both: the import dialog stays open on its result step with the reason, and the Sync page
  lists each agent's `warnings` under the result (before, both reported a plain success while the
  agent ran unsigned).
- **A cascade keeps the store's status.** `AgentCascadeService` mapped a refused agent save to
  `FAILED`, i.e. a 500 whose message never reached the body. A 400 stays a 400, and a 503 is the new
  `UNAVAILABLE` kind (503); the message carries the store's response body, since the loopback client
  reports only the status line. The OpenAPI texts of the cascade and `updateResourceUri` name both.
- **Manager.** A **Revoke** button per key (admins only, with a confirmation), a *Revoked* badge, the
  DID shown read-only, the page test for a known non-admin that the review found missing, and MSW
  audit fixtures in the backend's `v<n>:<Base64>` form. The legacy key (v0) keeps its row and its
  Revoke once generated keys exist (generating a key keeps `identity.publicKey`, which peers trust
  for v0 signatures; the row used to disappear). The "a save with signing on is refused" warning
  also shows when the newest key is revoked, and the revoke dialog says saves are refused until a
  new key exists or signing is off. It also says deleting the private key is best effort, that
  revoking the legacy v0 key removes it, and that a revoked versioned key is not yet refused by peer
  verification. Copy in all 11 locales.
- **Operator.** The system prompt explains revocation, that `identity` cannot be edited through a
  save, the cascade's signing 400/503, and the import/sync report; revision bumped.

### Upgrading: agents already stored with these flags

Before this branch the save-time check accepted `signInterAgentMessages` or
`requirePeerVerification` whenever `identity` held any non-blank string, and nothing could put a
private key in the vault. So an agent stored with `signInterAgentMessages: true` signs nothing, and
after the upgrade **every save of it is refused (400)** — a person's save, a workflow cascade, an
autonomy change, an API sync — until an admin either generates a key (**Generate signing key** in
the Manager, or `POST /agentstore/agents/{id}/signing-keys?version={current}`) or turns the flag
off. Find them with a query on `security.signInterAgentMessages: true` in the agents collection
(or table). An import or a sync onto such an agent turns the flag off and reports it, rather than
failing. Any `identity.keys` entry a person pasted before this branch is a key whose private half
someone outside EDDI may hold: review the keys of every agent a `requirePeerVerification` agent
checks, and revoke every entry EDDI did not generate — the legacy `identity.publicKey` (v0)
included, which generating a key does not remove. On this branch alone revoking the v0 key removes
it, but a revoked versioned entry still verifies in peer checks: the peer verifier does not read the
flag until group-signature verification, which builds on this change.

**Agents stored with `security.requirePeerVerification: true`.** Until now peer verification only
logged a failure. With group-signature verification, which builds on this change, such an agent
receives every peer entry that does not verify as a `[withheld ...]` placeholder — and since nothing
could sign before this branch, that is every entry another agent wrote. One such agent among a
group's members, moderator or facilitator also makes the group's records count only entries that
verify for every reader: the vote finds no options and records that it could not run, the
negotiation table stays empty, and the SERVICE judge and the retrospective see only signed entries —
for every member of that group. The only trace is ERROR logs. Find these agents with a query on
`security.requirePeerVerification: true`. To recover, generate a key and turn on
`signInterAgentMessages` for every agent that writes into those groups (members, moderator or
synthesizer, facilitator), or turn the flag off.

### Design decisions

- Identity is kept for **every** caller, admins included, not only for non-admins: one rule, and the
  endpoints that change keys are where the audit trail and the vault write are.
- Revocation is a flag, not a deletion of the entry: a verifier must recognise and refuse an entry
  signed under the key, which an unknown version would only report as "unknown key". The group
  verifier on this branch does not read the flag yet (that is the group-signing verification work);
  until it does, a signature made with a revoked versioned key still verifies. Deleting the vault's
  private key only stops EDDI signing with it (and is best effort); it does not stop anyone holding
  another copy of that private key from signing messages that verify.
- The newest key being revoked stops signing rather than falling back to an older key, matching the
  signer's rule for a key outside its window. `GroupSigningGuard` reads the flag before it signs, so
  this holds when the vault delete failed and the private key is still there. "Newest" is
  `AgentIdentity.signingKeyVersion()`, the newest version within `1..MAX_VERSION`, the same rule the
  validator applies: a legacy entry outside that range, revoked or not, neither picks the signing
  key nor silences a usable one.
- A malformed key that is revoked no longer blocks a save, so revoking is the way out for an agent
  stored with a planted entry.
- The import/sync report is a header and an `UpgradeResult` field, shown verbatim by one Manager
  component on the import dialog and the Sync page: the texts are EDDI's own and already name the
  way back, and `warnings` is a general list, not a signing flag.
- Generate and revoke treat a write whose outcome is unknown the same way, through one helper that
  reads the agent again and asks whether the change is there; only what to do when it is not
  differs (a generated key is discarded, a revoked one kept). Without it, a revoke that landed
  unacknowledged left the descriptor behind, every later save answered 404, and revoking again
  could not repair it. When the descriptor write itself fails, the endpoint answers 500 naming the
  new version, and a key change retried at that version (the version a 409 for the old one hands
  back) first moves a descriptor left exactly one version behind, then carries on. Only that shape
  is repaired; a missing descriptor, or one further behind, fails as before. An ordinary agent save
  at the new version does not repair it: retry the key change.

### Tests

`RestAgentStoreTest` (`IdentityIsEngineOwned`, `SigningKeyRevocation`, new `SigningKeyProvisioning`
and validator cases), `AgentPublicKeyTest`, `AgentSigningServiceTest`, `AgentCascadeServiceTest`,
`RestAgentStoreCascadeTest`, `UpgradeExecutorSyncTest`, `RestImportServiceCompatibleTest`,
`RestImportServiceRollbackAndCleanupTest` (including the first-promotion sync's `warnings`), the
Manager section, page, import-dialog, Sync-page and API tests, and a new
[`AgentSigningKeyIT`](../../src/test/java/ai/labs/eddi/integration/AgentSigningKeyIT.java) that runs
generate, save, sign-on, deploy, converse and revoke through the HTTP stack, response filters
included.

## refactor(hitl): one GuardText for the self-ungating heuristics (2026-11-08)

**Repo:** EDDI (`refactor/self-ungating-guard-text`)

### What changed and why

The two server-side guards around an approved tool call decided "does this request name an id?" with two different decodings. `SelfUngatingGuard` percent-decoded the raw arguments and also walked them as parsed JSON, a few levels into JSON-in-a-string. `ApprovedCallScope.targetsConversation`, which backs the self-conversation rule, percent-decoded the text and ran a hand-written JSON unescape over it. Given the same arguments, the two disagreed, and each one missed spellings the other caught:

- **Self-conversation rule missed:** an id inside a document carried as a JSON string (escaped once per level, so one unescape pass leaves the escape in place), and a JSON escape that was itself percent-encoded (`%5Cu0034`).
- **Self-ungating rule missed:** an escaped id in arguments that only a lenient parser accepts (single quotes, for example, or a leading comment). Its strict parse failed, or never started because the text did not begin with a bracket or a quote, so only the raw-text test ran.

Both now go through one package-private `GuardText` in `engine/hitl/tools`. It has three parts. `decodeLower` percent-decodes and lower-cases, and keeps the raw text when an escape is malformed. `containsId` does a trimmed, case-insensitive containment test. `haystack` builds the decoded raw text plus every key and string value from a JSON walk, and follows nested JSON strings up to four levels. The walk parses with every `JsonReadFeature` enabled: a parse can only add text to the haystack, so the most lenient parse is the safe one. It parses any text that starts with a character such a document can start with, comments (`/*`, `//`, `#`) and single quotes included, not only `{`, `[` and `"`. `ApprovedCallScope.jsonUnescaped` and `urlDecoded` are deleted. The lenient parse covers what `jsonUnescaped` covered for non-strict JSON. The only thing it no longer catches is unparseable text such as truncated JSON, and no receiver accepts that either.

Also fixed: `SelfUngatingGuard.collectNonEmptyKeyPaths` carried two stacked Javadoc blocks. The first one described `collectKeyPaths`, which had none, so it now sits on that method.

### Behaviour

Behaviour only gets stricter, in two places. The self-conversation rule (on resume and in the live tool loop through `ToolLoopResumer.uriTargetsConversation`) now refuses the two spellings listed above. The raw-argument self-ungating test now refuses escaped ids and keys in lenient JSON, including JSON that starts with a comment. No config, endpoint, MCP tool or default changed.

### Tests

`GuardTextTest` (new). Decoding: malformed percent escapes, a JSON-escaped key, the nested-string depth bound, non-JSON text and `containsId`. Agreement: `SelfUngatingGuard.refusalFromArguments` and `ApprovedCallScope.targetsConversation` must both see an id in seven escaped or encoded spellings, and both must miss another id. Before the change, 5 of the 7 spellings failed, in both directions. The existing `SelfUngatingGuard*Test`, `ApprovedCallScopeTest` and `ToolLoopResumerSelf*Test` pass unchanged.

**Files:** [`GuardText.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/hitl/tools/GuardText.java), [`SelfUngatingGuard.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/hitl/tools/SelfUngatingGuard.java), [`ApprovedCallScope.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/hitl/tools/ApprovedCallScope.java), [`GuardTextTest.java`](../../src/test/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/hitl/tools/GuardTextTest.java)

### Next

`ToolLoopResumer.argumentsTargetConversation` still runs its own JSON walk (`appendStrings`) after `uriTargetsConversation`. `uriTargetsConversation` already searches the full haystack, so that second pass is now redundant and can be removed. It was left in place because this PR is scoped to the two guard classes. Splitting the JSON-pointer and patch helpers out of `SelfUngatingGuard` into a `PatchGate` remains an optional follow-up.

## 🐛 fix(a2a): tool names within 64 characters, never empty; card cache keyed by credential (2026-11-07)

**Repo:** EDDI (`fix/a2a-tool-names`)

### What changed and why

- **A2A tool names over 64 characters.** The agent name and the skill id were each bounded at 64 characters, then joined, so a tool name reached 129. OpenAI, Anthropic and Gemini refuse a function name over 64, and one such name fails every request of the agent, not just the call to that tool: a 40-character configured agent name next to a 50-character peer skill id was enough, and a hostile peer card could do it on purpose. `A2AToolProviderManager` now makes every tool name in one place (`toolName`): each part sanitized, the whole cut to 64 with a short hash of the uncut name — the hash suffix `distinctNamePart` already used for skill ids, now shared — so two long ids that share a prefix stay two tools.
- **Empty A2A tool names.** Sanitizing keeps only `[a-z0-9_]`, so an agent named in a script other than Latin (`検索エージェント`) gave an empty name. `ToolSpecification` does not check the name, so the blank name reached the provider, which refuses it, and every request of the agent failed. With skills, the name lost its agent part, so two such peers collided on `lookup` and the second was skipped. A part that sanitizing empties now becomes `a2a_<hash of the original>` (`skill_<hash>` for a skill id).
- **A blank configured `name` now falls back to the card's name**, as an absent one always did. The Manager writes `""` once a typed name is cleared; that gave an empty or agent-less tool name before, and the shared `a2a` fallback after the change above, so a second such peer was skipped as a name collision.
- **Agent cards cached by URL alone.** A card fetched with one config's credential — possibly an authenticated card listing skills the peer shows only to that caller — was served for five minutes to every config naming the same URL with another credential or none, and its skills became their tools. The cache is now keyed on the URL plus a SHA-256 digest of the *unresolved* `apiKey`, as `McpToolProviderManager.cacheKey` does for MCP clients; configs naming one vault reference still share a card. An unauthenticated fetch keeps the bare URL as its key.
- The discovery branch for a credential or malformed-config failure now logs `A2A agent at … skipped: <reason>` instead of claiming every such failure was a credential problem (a card naming an endpoint on another origin lands there too).

### Design decisions

- **The 64-character cap is on the whole name, the per-part 64 stays.** The part bound exists for memory (an unbounded agent name was copied into every skill's tool name); the whole-name cap is what providers enforce. Each does its own job.
- **Fallback with a hash, not a bare `a2a`.** A bare fallback makes every non-Latin peer collide, and the second is silently skipped as a duplicate.
- **The digest helper is local, not a shared `RemoteCacheKey`.** Extracting one would edit `McpToolProviderManager`, outside this change; the codebase already keeps such SHA-256 helpers local to their class.

**Files:** [`A2AToolProviderManager.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/modules/llm/impl/A2AToolProviderManager.java), [`A2AToolProviderManagerTest.java`](../../src/test/java/ai/labs/eddi/modules/llm/impl/A2AToolProviderManagerTest.java), [`a2a-protocol.md`](../a2a-protocol.md)

## fix(signing): private-key cache expires; unused provisioning and rotation methods removed (2026-11-07)

**Repo:** EDDI (`fix/agent-signing-key-cache`)

### What changed and why

- **The loaded private key now expires after 60 s.** `AgentSigningService` kept a loaded agent
  signing key in a plain map for the life of the process. Only this node's own generate and delete
  paths evicted it, so a key replaced or removed in the vault any other way — on another node, or
  outside the service — was never picked up: the node kept signing with the old key, inter-agent
  self-verification against the new public key failed (the message went out unsigned), and audit
  entries carried signatures that no longer verified. The "no key" answer already expired after
  60 s; the "found" answer now does too. Both live in one Caffeine cache (`expireAfterWrite` 60 s,
  10,000 entries) on the existing test clock, replacing the two hand-rolled maps and the
  clear-everything size bound. The TTL constant is renamed `KEY_CACHE_TTL_NANOS` since it no longer
  covers only misses.
- **`generateKeyPair`, `generateKeyPairVersioned` and `rotateKey` are deleted**, with the
  eviction counter that guarded a race between a vault lookup and one of them. None had a
  production caller — `RestAgentStore.validateSecurityFlags` already said so — and
  [`architecture.md`](../architecture.md#cryptographic-agent-identity) claimed keys were
  "provisioned as part of agent creation", sending operators looking for a step that does not
  exist. The section now says what is true: nothing in EDDI provisions or rotates an agent signing
  key, the secrets API cannot write an `agent-signing-key:` name (its key-name pattern has no `:`),
  and an agent without a vault key signs nothing. `deleteKeyPair` and its version sweep stay. The
  deleted generators were the only code able to write such an entry, so making signing keys
  provisionable means bringing key generation back behind an admin operation.
- [`configuration-reference.md`](../configuration-reference.md) `eddi.audit.agent-signing-enabled`:
  the 60 s re-check now covers a replaced or removed key, not only a missing one.
- Comments that named the deleted methods now say what is true: `RestAgentStore.validateSecurityFlags`,
  `RestAgentStoreTest`, and `planning/manager-ui-handoff.md`, whose proposed "Generate Keypair" button
  now notes that it needs an admin endpoint first.
- **A vault outage now reaches signing after 60 s.** A loaded key used to outlive any vault failure;
  now, once it expires, a vault that cannot answer makes signing fail, so inter-agent messages go out
  unsigned (WARN) and audit entries carry no agent signature until the vault answers again. Nothing
  else changes: a vault error is never cached, and the vault shares the configuration database.

### Design decisions

- **Deletion over wiring an admin endpoint.** The alternative was a REST and MCP operation
  (`eddi-admin`) that calls the generators. That is a new capability surface — a route, an MCP
  tool, the operator prompt, docs — for a feature nobody has asked to provision yet; the methods
  were removed and later recovered once before (2026-05) without ever gaining a caller. The
  deletion is its own commit, so it can be reverted alone if the endpoint is wanted.
- **One cache for hits and misses** rather than a Caffeine cache beside the existing miss map:
  one TTL, one size bound, one eviction call, and `signIfKeyPresent`'s fast path is a single
  lookup.
- **Not `Cache.get(key, loader)`.** The vault read stays outside the cache's compute, as before,
  so a slow vault does not hold a map bin lock that blocks unrelated agents.

### Tests

`AgentSigningServiceTest`: a key replaced in the vault signs after the TTL and the old one no
longer does; inside the TTL the cached key signs with no second vault read; a key removed from the
vault elsewhere stops signing after the TTL; a key added for an agent just found keyless (unversioned
and rotated) signs after the TTL. The generator and rotation tests are gone; the remaining tests
provision keys by writing the vault entry directly, which is all the service reads.

**Files:** [`AgentSigningService.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/configs/agents/AgentSigningService.java),
[`AgentSigningServiceTest.java`](../../src/test/java/ai/labs/eddi/configs/agents/AgentSigningServiceTest.java),
[`architecture.md`](../architecture.md), [`configuration-reference.md`](../configuration-reference.md),
[`RestAgentStore.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/configs/agents/rest/RestAgentStore.java),
[`manager-ui-handoff.md`](../../planning/manager-ui-handoff.md)

## 🐛 fix(alerts): cap alert rules per agent; numeric match type selectable on empty values (2026-11-07)

**Repo:** EDDI (`fix/alert-rule-cap`)

### What changed and why

- **Alert rules per agent are capped.** `AlertRuleValidator` bounded the fields of one
  rule, but nothing bounded the number of rules, and every rule watching an agent is
  evaluated on each of its ratings and failed turns. New property
  `eddi.alerts.max-rules-per-agent` (default `50`, at least `1`): a create or update that
  would give an agent more rules listing it — enabled or not — is refused with 409
  `RULE_LIMIT_REACHED`, naming the agent (an every-agent rule: the every-agent count) and the property. The rules over every agent
  have a limit of the same size of their own (`IAlertRuleStore.countAllAgents`, in the
  MongoDB and PostgreSQL stores), so at most twice the limit watch one agent.
- **Only what a save adds is counted.** An agent the stored rule already listed, or an
  every-agent rule that already was one, is not counted again, so after the limit is
  lowered the existing rules stay editable and only new ones are refused. The check
  runs after the access checks, so a caller without `EDIT` learns nothing about another
  agent's rule count, and a test send is never counted.
- **Manager:** the rule editor shows the server's sentence for `RULE_LIMIT_REACHED`,
  as it does for `INVALID_RULE` (no new strings).
- **Manager: Number could not be chosen as an `OUTPUT_MATCH` value type on an empty
  value.** `OutputMatchBuilder` read the type back from the value; an unset number is
  `null`, which reads as Text, so choosing Number snapped back, and clearing a number
  field or typing a lone `-` flipped it to Text mid-edit. The chosen type is now held by
  the builder; a non-null value of another type (a stored rule opened in the editor, or
  the text value a switch to the actions source leaves) still decides it.
- **Platform Operator:** the alert section names the limit and the refusal; operator
  revision bumped.

### Design decisions

- One limit for both pools rather than counting the every-agent rules into each agent's
  total: an every-agent save would otherwise have to check every agent. The worst case
  per agent (twice the limit) is bounded and documented.
- A count before the insert, not a transactional reservation: two saves at the same
  moment can each pass once. Only editors and administrators write rules and evaluation
  runs on the alert worker, so the soft edge is not worth a lock.

**Files:** [`RestAlertRules.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/alerts/rest/RestAlertRules.java),
[`AlertSettings.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/alerts/AlertSettings.java),
[`IAlertRuleStore.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/alerts/IAlertRuleStore.java),
[`output-match-builder.tsx`](../../ui/manager/src/components/alerts/output-match-builder.tsx),
[`alerts.md`](../alerts.md#operating-alerts),
[`configuration-reference.md`](../configuration-reference.md#alerts)

## 🐛 fix(apicalls): preRequest updates template data in place; final-attempt failure keys; fire-and-forget batch continues (2026-11-07)

**Repo:** EDDI (`fix/api-call-executor-defects`)

### What changed and why

**`preRequest.propertyInstructions` no longer wipe the call's template data.**
`PrePostUtils.executePreRequestPropertyInstructions` ran the instructions and
then rebuilt the template data from conversation memory, returning a fresh map.
Everything the caller had put in the map was gone: the arguments an LLM tool
call merged in (`DELETE /items/{id}` went out as `DELETE /items/`), and in a
rule-triggered batch the `{responseObjectName}`, `…Error`, `…HttpCode` and
header values the call wrote, which the next call of the turn could no longer
template. An empty `propertyInstructions: []` list did the same. The method now
works like `runPostResponse`: the instructions update the caller's map in place
(each one already refreshed `{properties}` there), a plaintext a
`scope: "secret"` instruction vaulted is scrubbed from it, and the same map is
returned. `McpCallsTask`, which assigns the return value the same way, is fixed
by the same change; `LlmTask` ignored the return value, so it kept its map all
along and now also gets the scrub. `PrePostUtils` no longer needs `IMemoryItemConverter`; the
constructor parameter is gone. The HITL pinning predicate
(`canExecuteDivergeFromResolve`) is unchanged — a call with pre-request
instructions stays unpinnable — and its Javadoc now gives the real reason.

**A retried call's failure keys come from its final attempt.** On a non-2xx
response with `saveResponse`, every attempt wrote `{name}Error` and
`{name}HttpCode` to memory and to the template data, and nothing removed them:
a `503` followed by a successful retry left `weatherHttpCode=503` and the 503
body next to a good `weather`, where `postResponse`, output templates and the
next call of the turn all saw a failure. A `503` with a body followed by a
`500` with a blank one paired the first body with the second code. Now the
template data holds the current attempt's failure keys only (they are removed
before the next attempt, so a `responseValuePathMatchers` entry on
`{name}Error` still judges the response it decides about), and memory records
the final attempt's failure once, after the loop — as the `forEach` path does.
A matcher that reads the same keys through memory
(`memory.current.httpCalls.{name}HttpCode`) used to see each attempt because
every attempt was written to the live step output; the retry decision now gets
a copy of the template data with the attempt's failure keys under that path, so
it still sees the attempt it decides about while memory stays final-only.

**One failed request of a fire-and-forget batch no longer drops the rest.**
A `fireAndForget` call with `preRequest.batchRequests` sends its requests as
one background task. With `executeCallsSequentially`, a request that threw (a
timeout, a connection reset) ended that task, so every later request was never
sent; the only trace was a generic runtime error naming neither the call nor
the item. In the concurrent mode the same happened when a dispatch threw
synchronously. Each request is now sent on its own: a failure is logged with
the call name, its position (`request 2 of 5`) and a redacted reason, the rest
of the batch still goes out, and a summary line counts the failures. An
interrupted batch thread stops sending and says how many were left.

### Behaviour changes

- A `preRequest` property instruction with a `toObjectPath` now writes into the
  template data the request renders from; the rebuild used to discard it.
- Nothing a template read through the rebuilt map is lost: `{properties.x}` is
  refreshed in place by every instruction, and `{memory.current…}` is a live view
  of the current step, which already shows the property just set (under
  `memory.current.properties`) without a rebuild.
- A retry request can no longer template the previous attempt's
  `{name}HttpCode` / `{name}Error`. That was undocumented, and it is the
  divergence between the previewed and the sent request that the HITL pinning
  guard warns about.
- Memory gets one `{name}Error` / `{name}HttpCode` entry per call instead of one
  per failed attempt, written after the response headers rather than before.

**Files:** [`PrePostUtils.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/modules/apicalls/impl/PrePostUtils.java),
[`ApiCallExecutor.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/modules/apicalls/impl/ApiCallExecutor.java),
[`httpcalls.md`](../httpcalls.md) (`executeCallsSequentially` and `retryApiCallInstruction.maxRetries` rows);
tests `PrePostUtilsTest`, `PrePostUtilsDeepBranchTest`, `PrePostUtilsSecretScopeTest`,
`ApiCallExecutorTest`, `ApiCallExecutorPreRequestTest` (new), `ApiCallExecutorFireAndForgetBatchTest` (new),
and the constructor call in the other `PrePostUtils` users.

## 🐛 fix(channels): the channelId uniqueness check fails closed like the name check (2026-11-07)

**Repo:** EDDI (`fix/channel-id-uniqueness`)

### What changed and why

Two channel integrations must not bind the same `channelType:channelId`: the
router keys its routing map on it, so a second one silently takes over the
first one's channel. `validateUniqueChannelId` enforced that, but it swallowed
every failure: an entry it could not read was skipped at debug level, and a scan
that could not run at all logged "allowing save". One store timeout while
reading the integration that held `C12345` was enough to store a second
integration bound to `C12345`. The name check right below it already failed
closed (503) and skipped only a missing or undeserialisable document.

The three near-identical descriptor scans (channelId check, name check, and the
copy-name collector of `duplicateChannel`) are now one private walker,
`forEachOtherIntegration`, with the name check's error policy:

- a descriptor whose integration no longer exists is skipped;
- a stored document that no longer deserialises is skipped, so one corrupt
  entry cannot block every channel write;
- anything else (descriptors cannot be listed, an entry cannot be read right
  now) refuses the save with **503**, "Could not verify that the channel
  integration channelId is unique. Please retry."

### Behaviour changes

- Creating or updating an integration with a `channelId` answers 503 instead of
  saving when the uniqueness scan hits a transient store failure. Retrying
  succeeds once the store answers.
- Duplicating an integration answers 503 one step earlier (while picking the
  copy's name) on the same failure; the name check that followed already
  answered 503 for it.

### Known limit

The scan and its lock serialise saves on one node only; two nodes saving the
same `channelId` at the same moment can still both pass. Recorded in the
walker's Javadoc.

**Files:** [`RestChannelIntegrationStore.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/configs/channels/rest/RestChannelIntegrationStore.java),
[`RestChannelIntegrationStoreCrudTest.java`](../../src/test/java/ai/labs/eddi/configs/channels/rest/RestChannelIntegrationStoreCrudTest.java),
[`slack-integration.md`](../slack-integration.md)

## 🐛 fix(deployment): cluster wait never enters the sweep; tracking derived from the registry (2026-11-07)

**Repo:** EDDI (`fix/deployment-tracking`)

### What changed and why

Two defects in `AgentDeploymentManagement`, both in cluster mode.

- **A request waited behind the whole deployment sweep.** When a request reached a node
  before that node had deployed an agent another node had just deployed,
  `awaitClusterDeployment` ran the full sweep under its lock before it started its
  15 s wait. The request queued behind any running sweep, deployed every other agent the
  node was missing before its own, and a burst of requests ran one full sweep each. A
  version whose deployment had failed on the node cost every request the full 15 s,
  because it never became ready. The wait now starts its clock first, reads the
  deployment records and this node's registry once, and submits a deployment of just the
  missing versions of the requested agent to the runtime executor. Requests that arrive
  while that deployment is queued or running do not submit it again. A version in `ERROR`
  on this node is neither retried nor waited for, since the sweep owns retries and their
  backoff. While the startup migrations run or are deferred, nothing is deployed on
  demand, as before.
- **A version this node undeployed itself stayed "tracked".** The sweep kept a private
  list of the deployments it had confirmed READY and skipped them from then on. Only an
  undeploy that arrived as an event from another node removed an entry. A REST undeploy on
  this node, the daily retirement, the delete cascade and sub-agent teardown left the entry
  in place. When the same version was deployed again by a request on another node, this
  node's sweep found it tracked and never deployed it, and that node's share of the
  traffic waited 15 s and was answered as not ready until a restart. The sweep now starts
  by dropping every tracked row this node's registry does not hold READY. A registry that
  cannot be read drops nothing. The tracked set is a concurrent set, so an undeploy event
  no longer takes the sweep lock: one undeploy arriving during a long sweep used to stall
  every later cluster event on the node, cache invalidations included. The sweep reads
  the registry before the deployment records, so an undeploy (registry first, record
  second) that lands while the sweep runs is not taken for a deployment that went
  missing.
- **An undeploy-only teardown now marks the deployment record undeployed.**
  `teardown_agent` without `delete`, and the end-of-discussion cleanup when it only
  undeploys (`lifecyclePolicy: UNDEPLOY_ONLY`, or an agent whose origin cannot be
  verified), removed the sub-agent from this node's registry and left its record saying
  deployed. Once tracking follows the registry, the next sweep would deploy it again
  within about ten seconds; before, it came back at the next restart, and other nodes in
  a cluster never undeployed it. Both paths now flip the agent's `production` records to
  undeployed, as a REST undeploy does, so every node's sweep undeploys it too.

### Design decisions

- Tracking is derived from `IAgentFactory.getAllDeployedAgents` on every sweep instead of
  hooking every local undeploy path. The registry is the fact; the list was a second copy
  of it, the same lesson `AgentFactory` records for its `eddi_agents_deployed` gauge.
- The on-demand path reads the registry with `getAllDeployedAgents`, not `getAgent`, which
  blocks up to 60 s on a deployment in progress.
- Rejected: running `checkDeployments()` asynchronously from the request. It would still
  deploy every other missing agent before this one and still queue one sweep per request.

**Files:** [`AgentDeploymentManagement.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/runtime/internal/AgentDeploymentManagement.java),
[`IAgentDeploymentManagement.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/runtime/IAgentDeploymentManagement.java),
[`AgentDeploymentManagementClusterTest.java`](../../src/test/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/runtime/internal/AgentDeploymentManagementClusterTest.java),
[`AgentDeploymentManagementBranchTest.java`](../../src/test/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/runtime/internal/AgentDeploymentManagementBranchTest.java),
[`GroupLifecycleOps.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/internal/groups/GroupLifecycleOps.java),
[`TeardownAgentTool.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/modules/llm/tools/TeardownAgentTool.java),
[`clustering.md`](../clustering.md),
[`group-conversations.md`](../group-conversations.md)

## Documentation review: docs, root and UI guides, inline Javadoc (2026-11-07)

**Repo:** EDDI · **Branch:** `docs/documentation-review`

Checked every line of the 100 pages under `docs/` (not `archive/` or the changelog), the root guides (`AGENTS.md`, `README.md`, `CONTRIBUTING.md`, `GOVERNANCE.md`, `PRIVACY.md`, `SECURITY.md`) and the `ui/chat` / `ui/manager` guides against the code, `pom.xml`, `application.properties`, the workflows, the Dockerfile and `helm/`. Swept the Javadoc, OpenAPI text and tool descriptions of `src/main/java` for comments that contradict the code. Wrong claims were fixed in place, and missing config fields, endpoints, defaults and limits were added where a page already covers the feature.

**Notable corrections**

- MCP tool counts: 108 tools across 16 classes, 354 arguments of which 145 required (was 96 / 303 / 129 in `AGENTS.md`, `README.md`, [MCP server](../mcp-server.md), [MCP client](../mcp-client.md)).
- Building the image: `-Dquarkus.container-image.build=true` looks for a `Dockerfile.jvm` that no longer exists. The docs and the `mise` `docker-build` task now run `docker build -f src/main/docker/Dockerfile`, the same as CI.
- The Dev UI is at `/q/dev-ui` on Quarkus 3.
- [LLM parameters](../llm-parameters.md): the generator (`scripts/gen-llm-parameters-doc.py`) now renders each rule's `when`/`unless` condition and writes boolean defaults as `true`/`false`. Before this, conditional rejections read as unconditional.
- [Behavior rules](../behavior-rules.md): the "Welcome" API example could never fire. `tagMatches` reads `agent:tags`, which nothing writes, and the page now warns about it.
- CI and release pages: when Trivy, Gitleaks, CodeQL, the SBOM and fuzzing actually run; release candidates do not move `latest`; tags are not signed; the SBOM is a workflow artifact, not a release asset.
- Javadoc: broken `{@link}`/`@see` targets fixed, Javadoc blocks that had drifted off their members moved back, stale "package"/"LangChain"/`SafeHttpClient`/`@DefaultBean` wording updated, and MCP tool descriptions now say "workflow".
- `ConfigurationReferenceCoverageTest` now allows `EDDI_BACKEND_URL`, the Manager dev-server proxy variable that is now documented.

**Found, not fixed (code issues)**

- `DeploymentContextCondition.tagMatches` can never match: no code writes `agent:tags`.
- `DreamConfig.batchSize` / `maxUsersPerRun` and `ScheduleConfiguration.allowSelfScheduling` / `tenantId` are stored but never read.
- `IParserExtensionProvider` casts `lookupIfKnown` to `String`, so a JSON boolean would throw; it is also missing from the correction providers' `ExtensionDescriptor`.
- `helm/eddi/values.yaml` still says `nats.buildProfileImage` stopped being read with chart "< 2.5"; it was 2.7.0. Left alone, because a change under `helm/` needs a chart version bump.
- `McpConversationTools.buildConversationResponse` builds `agentResponse` only from output items that are `Map`s with a `text` key. Whether a live turn's snapshot still holds `TextOutputItem` objects (so the field would be missing) was not verified; `docs/mcp-server.md` keeps documenting the field.

## Full Java comment review: every file in src/main/java (2026-11-07)

**Repo:** EDDI · **Branch:** `docs/javadoc-full-review` (stacked on `docs/documentation-review`)

The earlier documentation review swept Javadoc by package and only partly covered it. This pass reads all of `src/main/java`, 1,811 files and about 6.7 MB of comments. The files were split into 24 slices of equal comment volume and each slice went to its own reviewer. Every comment line, Javadoc block and documentation string was checked against the code it describes: OpenAPI `@Operation`/`@Parameter`/`@APIResponse`, langchain4j `@Tool`/`@P`, MCP `@Tool`/`@ToolArg`, and `ExtensionDescriptor` text. Wrong comments were fixed. Four slices had read their largest files through a filtered comment view, and those 132 files went to eight more reviewers for a full read.

**What changed:** about 400 comment fixes across 384 files. A lexer-level comparison that strips comments from the old and new sources shows that no code changed: a draft also corrected 31 files' description and message strings, but those were taken out again in review (see below), so the branch changes comments only. Typical fixes:

- Javadoc that had drifted onto the wrong member (onto a constant, a field, or stacked on another block) moved back to the member it describes.
- `{@link}`/`@see` targets that no longer exist, or that named the wrong signature.
- Stale names:
  - `AgentOrchestrator.*` methods now in `ToolLoopRunner`, `ToolObjectReflector`, `ToolApprovalGateSupport` and the `*ToolsProvider`s;
  - `ConversationCoordinator`, `SafeHttpClient`, `dataFactory.createData`;
  - `@DefaultBean`/`@LookupIfProperty` wording where `DataStoreProducers` now selects the store.
- Wrong defaults, limits and counts, for example:
  - the tool sources now number eight with `team`;
  - Slack retries on 429/500/502/503/504;
  - the connection cache TTL is one minute, not five;
  - there are seven discussion presets, not six;
  - the `PostgresStartupSchemaLock` key decodes to `EDDISCHG`.
- Stale vocabulary: package→workflow and "LangChain"→LLM in comments.
- `@since 6.6.0`→`6.7.0` on the alert stores, which first shipped in tag 6.7.0.

**Strings left for a follow-up.** The draft also rewrote OpenAPI `@Operation`/`@APIResponse` descriptions, MCP `@Tool`/`@ToolArg` descriptions, langchain4j `@P`/`@Tool` texts, and log and error messages in 31 files (for example `list_conversations` listing all six states, `approve_group_phase` naming `taskApprovalsJson`, `delete_user_data` listing what the cascade deletes, the team-cadence 400 message naming `/groupstore/groups/{groupId}/workspace/cadences`). Those strings are code: the model and API clients read them. The review took them out so that this branch stays comment-only, which a lexer comparison against `main` now proves. They belong in their own PR.

**Review fixes (CodeRabbit was waived by the repository owner for this PR, which it skips as too large):** comments that the rewrite got wrong or left incomplete. `ConversationHitlService` (it never calls `say` on the facade), `GroupHitlCoordinator` (`planHashStrict`), `HitlConfigValidation` (pause-reason length, `maxRevisionsPerTurn` range), `GdprComplianceService` (where the conversation ids are resolved, and which steps use them), `UserDataExport` (attachment metadata covers all of the user's conversations), `SharedKvCache` (RAG ingestion statuses), `NatsConnectionManager`, `JetStreamEventBus` (`CACHE_CLEAR` lane), `ClusterConversationCoordinator` (when the forwarder runs), `HighValueSurfaceGuard` (the k8s auth overlay and Helm), `DisabledAuthController` (`authorization.enabled` follows OIDC), `VaultMasterKeyStrength` (`eddi.vault.allow-weak-master-key`), `GroupSigningGuard` (WARN versus ERROR), `DirectoryUser`, `IRestResourceSharing`, `RestAuditStore`, `RemoteApiResourceSource` (no plain `RuntimeException` exists), `GroupConversationEventSink` (nothing produces an award decision yet), `LegacyDocumentMigrations` (import skips the memory transform), `EmailOwnerNotifier` and `SecretValueScrubber`.

**Merged with `main` (2026-11-17).** Files only the stacked docs commit touched take `main`'s final #1342 version. In the 14 conflicting Java files `main`'s code was kept exactly, with this branch's comment only where it still describes that code. Comments `main`'s newer code had made stale were corrected in `Conversation` (eight bookmark fields), `TeamCadenceService`, `TaskForceEngine`, `GroupConversation`, `RestImportService`, `RagIngestionService`, `UpgradeExecutor`, `PiiRedactionGuardrail` (it also runs on tool-call arguments), `DreamService`, `RestAgentEngineStreaming` (`tool_result` carries `ok` and a redacted summary since #1285) and `GroupHitlCoordinator` (`announceUserEvents`). With comments stripped, the branch's Java is identical to `main`'s.

**Left alone:**
- The LLM task's `"Lang Chain"` descriptor label and a "package" log line in `RestAgentStore`, both asserted by tests.
- The `DeploymentContextCondition` comments, which labsai/EDDI#1337 rewrites.

**Code bugs found by this pass** are fixed test-first on the separate branch `fix/docs-review-findings`, not here:
- `LifecycleInterruptedException` drops its cause.
- The `ClusterConfig.redactUserInfo` regex uses `"\s"`, which in a Java string is a literal space, not "any whitespace".
- `ContractedWordNormalizer` expands "won't" to "would not".
- `RemoteApiResourceSource` returns 500 instead of 502 for an unlisted agent.
- The vault reset hint omits `?confirm=true`.
- `AgentStoreClientLibrary` uses the wrong logger category.
- `CounterweightService` never reaches its unknown-level warning.
- Suspected, still to be checked: `PostgresHealthCheck` activation on MongoDB deployments, and a layout inconsistency in the classifier guardrail's `TOOL_CALL` phase.

## 🐛 fix(email): pseudonymised audit, constructor-injected collaborators, bounded reply cache, one retry policy (2026-11-07)

**Repo:** EDDI (`fix/email-channel`)

### What changed and why

- **Audit entries name a mail address only by its keyed pseudonym.** A refused inbound mail (`email.inbound`) wrote
  `senderHash` and a sent, failed or bounced reply (`email.reply`) wrote `recipientHash`: an unkeyed SHA-256 next to
  the address's domain, in a write-once ledger erasure cannot reach. Anyone holding a list of candidate addresses at
  that domain could hash them and tell whose mail it was. Both entries now carry `senderPseudonym` /
  `recipientPseudonym` from `GdprPseudonymizer` — the keyed token HITL notifications (`email.notify`) already wrote
  under that name — and the bounce-suppression log line names the address the same way. The suppression list keeps
  its SHA-256: it is a lookup key, not audit output. Entries written before this change keep their `*Hash` field. The
  token is keyed with the audit ledger's key, or salted per deployment; only a deployment with neither falls back to
  an unsalted hash.
- **`EmailInboundProcessor` takes every collaborator through its constructor.** The reply sender and the audit ledger
  were field-injected and documented as "null in tests, and then no reply is ever attempted"; production code branched
  on `replies != null` three times, so a construction site that forgot them silently turned conversation-mode replies,
  group replies and bounce handling off. They are final constructor parameters now, with the pseudonymizer, and the
  null branches are gone; whether an integration replies is still `settings.replies()`.
- **The group-reply cache is bounded without forgetting everything.** A group reply's rendered request lives in the
  node's memory for its retries. The map was cleared wholesale once it reached 1,000 entries, and entries left only
  when the row ended on this node, so a row another node's sweep finished stayed forever; in a cluster the map filled
  until a clear dropped every reply still mid-retry, and each of those ended `FAILED (CONTENT_UNAVAILABLE)`. It is a
  Caffeine cache now: past 1,000 one entry is evicted, and an entry expires once its row cannot be retrying any more
  (`(lease + max backoff) × max-attempts`).
- **One retry policy for the email ledger's outbound rows.** The capped exponential backoff was copied into the HITL
  notifier, the reply sender and the mailbox poller, and the "fail, or reschedule and count a retry" branch four times;
  the copies had drifted — an unexpected (non-SMTP) reply failure was rescheduled but never counted in
  `eddi.email.outbound{kind=reply,outcome=retry}`. `LedgerRetry` holds the backoff and that branch; the three classes
  use it, and the reply sender now counts that retry.

### Design decisions

- `LedgerRetry` is a small instance (ledger, max attempts, base) rather than static helpers with nine parameters: it
  also owns the `max-attempts` / `retry-backoff` defaults both senders normalised separately. The send flows around it
  (retention, the pending gauge, content rebuilding) differ and stay in their classes — one shared worker would be a
  template method over two unlike flows.
- The cache's expiry is per write, not per access: the bound is "the longest a row can stay retryable", which is a
  property of the row's age, and a direct executor makes size eviction happen on the writing thread.

**Files:** [`LedgerRetry.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/integrations/email/ledger/LedgerRetry.java),
[`EmailInboundProcessor.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/integrations/email/EmailInboundProcessor.java),
[`EmailReplySender.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/integrations/email/reply/EmailReplySender.java),
[`HitlEmailNotifier.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/integrations/email/notify/HitlEmailNotifier.java),
[`EmailMailboxPoller.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/integrations/email/EmailMailboxPoller.java),
[`email-integration.md`](../email-integration.md)

### What's next

- `EmailInboundSweeper` still field-injects its `EmailReplySender` the same way; it was outside this change.
